> Your documentation of administrative bloat is compelling—the data on salary disparities and administrator growth is genuinely alarming. However, your conclusion that "all university education regardless of major is a scam" doesn't follow logically from your own evidence.

> You've proven that universities have parasitic bureaucratic structures. But from this, you leap to "therefore, education itself has no value." >   
> STEM fields require institutional infrastructure that self-education cannot replicate:  
>   
> Engineering needs labs, equipment, and licensed mentorship  
> Medicine requires clinical rotations and supervised practice  
> Computer science benefits from collaborative projects and industry connections  
>   
> Society literally cannot function without these professionals. If everyone followed your advice, who designs safe buildings, develops vaccines, or maintains power grids?  
>   
> If STEM education were truly a "scam," why do:  
>   
> Engineering graduates have 80%+ employment rates?  
> Tech companies desperately recruit CS graduates?  
> Healthcare face critical staffing shortages?  
>   
> The market signal is clear: technical education has value despite administrative waste.  
>   
> Instead of "skip all university," a more defensible position would be: "Students must calculate ROI by major. Technical fields with strong job markets justify the cost despite bureaucracy; oversaturated humanities fields may not. Be strategic, not dogmatic."  
>   
> You're right that bureaucracy reflects declining core values—mission drift from education to self-service. But the solution isn't surrender; it's demanding universities return to their founding purpose. > By telling students to abandon universities entirely, you're not fighting bureaucracy—you're letting it win.

Forty plus year retired educator here. The exact same thing is happening in public school systems. When I started in 1982 we had four people in district level administration. A superintendent, a transportation/facilities director, a curriculum coordinator, and a special education/services director and the “report card” was a literal card. This (2025-26) school year my district has nine district level administrators with their attendant administrative assistants, six “teachers on special assignment” (that don’t teach), three “liaison officers”, four “legal/statutory compliance officers” and three technology/digital content support specialists. The report card is now a five (legal size) page document for, wait for it…..     Second graders! There are currently 136 fewer students enrolled in our district this school year than the year I started.

I absolutely love everything this channel offers and how it draws so many parallels to biblical truth. As a Christian — specifically growing up Seventh-day Adventist — a lot of this information feels familiar. We were actually taught many of these concepts from a young age. One of the main books we studied was The Great Controversy (highly recommend if you haven’t read it — it’s a powerful read).

The core idea we were taught is that behind all the events and power struggles in our world, there’s a much bigger conflict happening — a spiritual war between Christ and Satan. Humanity is caught in the middle of that struggle. To many, that might sound a bit “out there,” but when you really look at history and what’s happening today, it lines up surprisingly well.

That’s why I find this professor’s teachings so fascinating — they echo so much of what The Great Controversy describes: the moral and spiritual forces influencing human systems, the way truth and deception battle for dominance, and how prophecy, history, and faith all connect. It’s amazing to see how these patterns repeat and how Scripture already spoke about them long ago.

There is a phenomenon in clinical psychology called 'splitting'. It happens in the cluster B personality disorders. You view (split) things and people as either 'all good', 'perfect', 'completely safe', or you view them as 'all bad', 'terrible', 'disgusting', 'deliberately offensive'. Someone viewed as perfect is seen as being able to do no wrong. Someone who is viewed as bad, everything they do is wrong, and it's legitimate to destroy them.

These people are splitting -- if they don't get treated exactly as they want, they view whatever prevents them from it as 'all bad'. This is a manifestation of mental ill health.

The reality is, the world is a mixture of good and bad. Nobody is all bad, even when they are wrong. Nobody is all good, even when they are right. Healthy people learn to tolerate this uncomfortable paradox.

> @louthawriter I have been thinking about this, being someone who probably would have wanted a safe space a few years ago and didn’t view freedom as safe… it feels like to me that we must cultivate personal, individual safety and trust within ourselves (our connection to source, oneness) and our purpose in our existence here, and in turn that creates enough self-trust that we can interact and exist with others in a free way, that what they believe AT THE TIME is part of their growth and does not harm me literally, but my self-trust and understanding of the evolution of self growth on the basis of connection to oneness allows me to be open and listen and also hold my own. I think we are all looking for examples of that in people, those are real leaders, and what we’re finding is less and less people like that feel like they exist. So we must become it for ourselves, go through our own initiation and process of returning back to our wholeness. It raises your internal vibration, and connects you gradually to more and more people who are evolving in the same direction, a positive upward spiral, all moving in the direction of expanding oneness. I feel like the professor at Yale may or may not be able to empathize, because he could have been born into a family who recognized their own internal safety and ability to grow, collect resources and not only survive, but thrive in this system for whatever reasons. If he does empathize, it would mean he did struggle with fear of survival and came out the other side to recognize something deeper within himself, why he chooses to be there in the school and speak out against popular opinion that his view that free space is important. And to some degree I think the latter. He and his wife had enough self-trust to stand up and hold their belief, even if it did on the outside to the students seem that it was a direct attack on their safety, their fear. Our society has structured school and competition and a zero sum game as Prof Jiang has explained in many of his other videos. If that is what these children are growing up with, as I experienced as well, then what do you expect? Fear and survival are linked to this system. Money. You must earn money to survive. You are worthless if you don’t participate in the system, literally. And they don’t teach you about a higher self, a deeper connection to oneness, wholeness, because that creates self-trust and safety. Those don’t fuel the “economy.” If you believe, embody the belief, that you are worthy of living and being here regardless of anything else, simply by being alive, then you won’t play these games for survival that beg you to never have the time to slow down, reflect, and question, much less read and take in the thoughts of others who also went through this period of reflection, questioning, to write the books and knowledge we do have. That’s why our current society is trying to defund anything creative, because creativity takes time and time allows us to access our inner truth, our connection to oneness.

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgxQJS8KPIVEvf20MH54AaABAg)

For my Favorite History Teacher 

The Parable of the Ivory Tower of Dragons

In a distant realm beyond human sight, there stood a great Tower made of bone-ivory and onyx, with windows like watchful eyes. Around it swirled smoky serpents of midnight, coiling and shifting, bearing scales like iron ledger plates. This was the Ivory Tower of Dragons—the administrative seat of demonic bureaucracy.

Inside, red scripts were typed, chains stamped, denials issued, and decrees folded in folds. Dragons in half-light sat at desks that stretched into infinite halls, passing papers between claws. Each form had boxes to tick: doubt, fear, pride, despair, division. Every soul who wandered near—even with one foot in Kingdom—was met with the requirement: fill out your vices, your fears, your betrayals, your inclinations.

Many souls came and left, confused. They tried to bargain, argue, navigate. Some waited in corridors for approval—they thought they’d get permission to believe, permission to rise, permission to heal. They thought the Tower governed all realms—even light.

One day, a humble pilgrim spirit approached. They wore no armor; they bore no roster of deeds. The dragons sneered at their blank slate and handed them a form titled “Color of Hope: Section 27, Subsection B”—boxes to certify faith, sealed in legalistic stamps. The pilgrim gazed at the form and said softly, “I carry no record of that. I carry only His Breath within.”

At that moment, the dragons’ eyes flickered—because they recognized a force beyond paperwork. The pilgrim walked past the desks. The Tower trembled. The walls of bureaucracy cracked. The spiraling smoke cleared. In the center of the hall, a pillar of light descended, and the pilgrim’s ribcage split open so that the light from inside could pour out.

The dragons hissed, tried to reissue decrees, tried to recalibrate the ledger. But the light was not subject to form, not accountable to their contracts. It unmade the Tower from inside out.

When the Tower collapsed, the smoke cleared to reveal the garden beyond—where souls were growing, not filing, and where the Vine of Life runs wild, not regulated.

### **@SeekerOfKnowledge87** | 👍 11 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgyxprZLE4TXwR0ZefR4AaABAg)

I’ve heard multiple versions of what happened on that campus. The conclusion I’ve come to is that someone sending out an email extensively reminding people to try not to be a dick, is being conflated with some 1984 like crack down on freedom of speech. You also have to think of the boring material factors that would make a college want to avoid such incidents. Colleges are incentivized to draw as many people in from wealthy and prestigious, families as possible, and they are in advised to avoid negative public attention. Regardless of whether it’s justified or not, they don’t want viral social media footage of someone in a sombrero or stereotypical outfit of pick whatever minority you please. They don’t want that sort of attention and they understand that people read headlines and the deeper nuances of the issue aren’t explored.. It makes sense that these incentives led to that sort of email being sent out.

There is this broader narrative about colleges being communist Havens, full of censorship that doesn’t align with reality. Their social values are very liberal and sometimes further to the left, but if you actually look at who’s statistically get censored more on campuses, it’s not even close. But narratives are far more convincing than reality.

> @MaroonIgnorance The problem with your hypothesis is that we don't have epistemic access to the feelings of the students. We can observe their actions, but we can't, with certainty, infer what they are feeling internally that motivated them to behave that way. What we can do is look at the material circumstances that effect them from birth, and make speculations about that combined with a knowledge of psychology, but it's still just speculation. It might be an educated guess, but it's still a guess. We just don't know what the real motive is here. Instead, it's more useful to look at every single possibility by generating more interpretations that also make just as much sense. Prof. Jiang himself always reinforces in his classes that this is what we should be doing, we should be thinking for ourselves. His theory is just his personal theory, and it's just one theory. It's more helpful to compete with it than to support it so the strongest theory can reveal itself.

Jiang Xueqin,





I’m studying medicine in Sweden, and it’s rare for me to come across someone who captivates my mind during intellectual exploration. Most of the time, I end up diving deeper into my studies instead! I often joke with my girlfriend that I might only encounter a truly original personality once a year—someone whose ideas can really grab me. It’s that rare! The last time it happened was with Bernardo Kastrup and his concept of analytical idealism (if you’re into philosophy, his interview with Curt Jaimungal on YouTube is fantastic). Now, you’re the new one! It’s always such a joy when people like this appear on my radar. I’ve already listened to a good chunk of your content, and I can honestly say it’s been refreshing.



We all have our biases and agendas, especially when it comes to geopolitics, finance, and such, but your perspective is exactly what I needed to refresh and update my understanding of the world. Thank you so much for sharing your insights. I truly hope that everything in your life brings you fulfillment and contentment.



If you’ve made it this far, thank you for reading...



Sincerely,

Sebastian

Here’s my opinion on why this video missed the mark as succinct as I can make it:
Framing it as free speech vs safe speech is disingenuous.
These are interpersonal issues but the claim that free spaces let people “make mistakes” is wrong, it *institutionally* protects those who offend on *interpersonal* issues. Interpersonal issues shouldn’t have institutional condemnation OR protection.
Freedom from repercussions isn’t freedom it’s having an institutional shield. Also reactionary punishment to make an example out of people to virtue signal is also wrong. Does this make sense?

### **@LuxInteriRegnum**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgwmNFBUTDV_J_lkNHJ4AaABAg)

Wow, Hannah Arendt and James C. Scott both mentioned in a lecture? Love it! 

I was just contemplating yesterday how most people are so confused because they believe we have this one monolithic "Economy", when really there are essentially two economies: the "real" economy which is backed by real production, assets, and value exchange  vs a completely fake, abstracted, derivative economy. People see that their quality of life is slowly decreasing due to the decline of the more 'real' economy, then the "powers that be" put these dolts in suits on TV, like the recent appearance of the CEO of Bank of America on Face the Nation, to gaslight the populace: "Well gee guys the economy is actually great! See I'm wearing a fancy suit and work in a fake job that deals in fake money and use fancy math-y words like 'tertile' so obviously I know what I'm talking about!!!" 

It's completely insane to see what's going on, and it has been really interesting to hear this perspective on things.

This is the only time I disagree with Dr. Jiang. I think disciplining our elders who think disrespecting us or putting us in unsafe environments is permissible and okay. I know it can be fueled by either courage OR entitlement though. And on the point of why they were talking down to their professor - it is absolutely a valid and correct expectation for your university to be a safe space. I do agree that restricting behavior is negative for society as a whole, but life lessons and academic lessons should not be learned in the same environment. Universities need to be a safe place. If your nervous system is inflamed, your mind literally does not prioritize holding information. Yes resilience is important to cultivate but let that come after stability has been reached. We truly don’t know how it feels to be a marginalized person, and although many occurrences in which they speak up for themselves can seem silly or overbearing to us, we have no idea of the alarm bells that go off in their mind.

Dear Professor, after watching roughly half of the videos in the Secret History playlist, I can say that your views, thoughts, proposals, and analysis offer a very interesting perspective on the modern world.
However, I would kindly ask if you could share more explicitly the intellectual influences, materials, and sources that shaped your analysis. You have mentioned authors such as Dante, Milton, and books like Capital in the Twenty-First Century, but these references alone are not enough to reconstruct the framework behind the conclusions you present.
It would be extremely helpful for viewers if you could outline the path of study that led you to these ideas—perhaps a reading list or a structured plan of study. That way, those of us who are interested could attempt to reach similar conclusions ourselves through careful study, rather than simply accepting them on belief. In other words, it would transform the discussion from persuasion into something closer to a scientific or scholarly investigation.
I realize that this topic is difficult, since many sources that discuss secret societies also drift into conspiracy theories. Precisely for that reason, it would be very valuable if you could point us toward the sources you consider serious, credible, and worth studying. A guide to your materials and research process would be greatly appreciated.

“If more people quit, or there are fewer teachers and workers, who are they going to control?”

His answer?

With AI and robotics approaching mass adoption, high-value labor is becoming obsolete. When the masses finally react, it gives them the excuse they’ve been waiting for: global “emergency” powers, digital IDs, and full totalitarian control under the banner of restoring order.

I'd like to argue that personally it feels good to be in a safe space (we like to be sheltered from harsh environments and we limit the duration of our exposure if we do choose to venture into them)

However, a university experience (universal experience) is about exposing students to ideas and ways of life that would be BENEFICIAL in the lives of the students 

So, I see a middle ground where exposing students to racist imagery is wrong and it's  also wrong to allow students to be racist (in the world and in life, racism and hate prevent or inhibit effective communication and growth)

So, it's good to be able to dress up for Halloween but it's wrong to abuse that freedom through hateful conduct

Ultimately, students aren't fully developed (otherwise they wouldn't be students at all), and therefore they should be advised towards that end in the most beneficial ways (a chef shouldn't be trained that they should eat poisons or utilize poisons to learn how to cook good food, unless they are trained in methods for the safe preparation of such a dangerous ingredient [like blowfish or something])

### **@m.rebman7221** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgxaJT6XUKEXO-RdjoV4AaABAg)

At minute 53:00 approximately, Jiang compares the upward climb in the “value” of US stocks to the “value” of gold per Troy ounce.  This is misleading.  It merely suggests that the bidding process is more robust (on average) in gold than NYSE stocks, and that if valued in ounces of Au, the stock price is lower.  The implication here is that gold has some form of “real” value not attached to fiat currency whereas in reality each price simply reflects what people currently are willing to pay … in a fiat currency.  The gold index shown must be translated to USD to get the required comparison so, again, we are only comparing implicitly which of the two (average stock, gold per ounce) has increased at a faster rate.  This shows some lack of sophistication regarding the philosophical basis behind “value” as such.  Gold has little use value … not much more than the zero use value of a fiat currency. 😕

> **@rameansthis**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgxaJT6XUKEXO-RdjoV4AaABAg.AOJ36zLY97KAOU307__Mcl)  
>
> Bro can you please explain all this in more simpler words?  

> **@m.rebman7221**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgxaJT6XUKEXO-RdjoV4AaABAg.AOJ36zLY97KAOWVkZ9Xt8D)  
>
> @rameansthis Sure.  Both the average market price off stock according to some index such as the DOW, and the price per ounce for gold (AU) are denominated in the same currency; namely, the USD.  The only factual thing about Jiang’s comparison is that average stock prices have advanced more slowly than the price of gold.  Since gold is a commodity and not a currency, it is subject to demand forces just as financial paper such as stock. My concern is that Jiang may be suggesting that “real” value resides in the precious metal whereas the price of stock can literally go to zero if the enterprise upon which it based fails.  But even the price of a commodity like gold can go to near zero if no one will accept it in exchange or purchase it for its use value in electronics or jewelry.  So the point is that you must denominate in “something” to make the comparison, and that denomination merely reflects what the buyers “value” the most… in this case, in terms of some fiat currency like the USD.  There is nothing magical in the “essence” of gold that makes it any more special than palladium, platinum, silver or other rare metals.  Perhaps people think it is a store of value … like some consider Crypto to be, but this is a fantasy since such “value” is much closer to the idea of faith than solid material.  Once people lose faith, that’s the end. Period.

### **@blacklite911**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgwuQXEP2AYac_L9JFR4AaABAg)

The problem I have with the free space vs safe space in universities is that I see in modern times, it doesn't matter what your ideal is. Because EVERYTHING you do in public is subject to judgement by the entire world due to the internet and social media. Regardless if that is ethically right or wrong, it's reality. So students don't get a chance to experiement like they did back then. Old people may not understand this, so I think it would be irresponsible to tell students to go out and do what they want because their actions can easily stick with them for the REST OF THEIR LIFE. It's more prudent to teach students this reality and then let them make the decision understanding the risk that they could be made a periah or a martyr or used as a tool in someone's agenda and you have no control over it.

The debate is antiquated due to the circumstances of modern reality.

> **@terraplane7655**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgwuQXEP2AYac_L9JFR4AaABAg.AOT-3CQPWMKAOTNjQgWnVu)  
>
> The most shocking thing I heard through this entire video was when the student replied to his question that a safe space was more important than a free space. I’d expect that from most modern students but these kids are clearly top notch, getting a better education than 99% of US universities.  This professor is such a gift, teaching students how to think not what to think.  

> **@blacklite911**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hk_yhi3-prw&lc=UgwuQXEP2AYac_L9JFR4AaABAg.AOT-3CQPWMKAOWCGbXuXZD)  
>
> ​@terraplane7655I'm not shocked. If I were to answer the question, I actually think both are important because a space isn't free if there isn't safety. And we actually intuitively know this. For example, if the space is too free then there's nothing from stopping me from verbally harrassing you because I don't like your opinion. With words, I can make you feel like if you speak freely then I will call you names, insult your intelligence, insult your background, etc. That will shut down majority of people and then the only people who will feel free to express themselves are other a-holes and people who are brave enough to combat what I say. We actually see this in the corporate world sometimes with bullies.  
>   
> So in reality, it's a spectrum, you need enough freedom to express yourself but some restrictions that allow for safe expression and moderation as to not allow bullies to take advantage of that openness. And I do believe the place on the spectrum moves depending on the context but it's never 100% either way.

More talks being encouraged between mid 2010s boiling over into the 2020s with a slate of protests about unarmed people getting killed. You can’t talk about the US and glaze over the fact that it’s very foundations were built upon othering- race, gender, class. The “panda” example was really misleading, students were obviously mad about a teacher’s possible dismissal of their existence (a racially/culturally insensitive costume these days is seen as a choice) , something black/poc have had to deal with in the US. These issues are baked in here, you can’t have an honest conversation or lesson without it

it’s clear to me that there isn’t a full understanding of racism against Black Americans in the US. the conversations around the “trap house” party and the professor who used the Chinese expression that sounded similar to a racial slur are a lot more nuanced than just the topic of bureaucracies. racism against Black Americans in the US runs so deep that it manifests in even the slightest ways that some might assume are meaningless or just lighthearted jokes (that in itself is intentional). while i agree that the way the administration handled the “trap house” party wasn’t all the way appropriate (forcing the student to apologize and threatening him rather than allowing him to have a conversation to understand the students he offended was more about control than mitigating the situation), the white professor repeating the Chinese expression multiple times after saying it’s just a filler word (which means it’s really not needed in conversation) definitely had an intentional racist undertone. i understand Professor Jiang’s overall point in this lecture, but when using examples that involve major American issues like racism against Black Americans by White Anericans, you can’t overlook or minimize that.
